# Complete Static Question Catalog

This catalog is the post-audit snapshot of every instantiated static question used by the Round 0 diagnostic, lesson practice, progress checks, and full-length tests. It contains **650 questions**: **245 English**, **223 Math**, **182 Reading**, and **0 Science**. Corrected fields include their exact pre-audit text under **Fixed from**.

Science is listed explicitly with zero entries because the current core schema and static banks expose only English, Math, and Reading.

## English

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-roof-1

**Stimulus:** [1] At first, the flat roof above the school gym seemed useful only to pigeons. [2] Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there, the principal was skeptical. [3] The roof, which had recently been reinforced could hold the added weight. [4] Students began by carrying up shallow planters, soil, and seedlings. [5] Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly. [6] The class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil. [7] These two changes reduced evaporation; as a result, the herbs survived the hottest weeks. [8] By September, the garden supplied basil and mint to the culinary arts class. [9] It also gave science students a place to measure how shade, wind, and soil depth affected plant growth.

**Question:** Which revision of sentence 2 correctly joins its clauses?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there, the principal was skeptical.
- **b.** Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there; the principal was skeptical. **(correct)**
- **c.** Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there the principal, was skeptical.
- **d.** Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there, being skeptical was the principal.

**Correct answer:** b — Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there; the principal was skeptical.

**Explanation:** Both sides are independent clauses, so a semicolon correctly joins them without a coordinating conjunction.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-roof-2

**Stimulus:** [1] At first, the flat roof above the school gym seemed useful only to pigeons. [2] Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there, the principal was skeptical. [3] The roof, which had recently been reinforced could hold the added weight. [4] Students began by carrying up shallow planters, soil, and seedlings. [5] Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly. [6] The class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil. [7] These two changes reduced evaporation; as a result, the herbs survived the hottest weeks. [8] By September, the garden supplied basil and mint to the culinary arts class. [9] It also gave science students a place to measure how shade, wind, and soil depth affected plant growth.

**Question:** Which revision of sentence 3 uses punctuation correctly?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The roof which had recently been reinforced, could hold the added weight.
- **b.** The roof, which had recently been reinforced, could hold the added weight. **(correct)**
- **c.** The roof which had recently, been reinforced could hold the added weight.
- **d.** The roof, which had recently been reinforced could hold, the added weight.

**Correct answer:** b — The roof, which had recently been reinforced, could hold the added weight.

**Explanation:** The nonessential clause can be removed and therefore needs a comma on both sides.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-roof-3

**Stimulus:** [1] At first, the flat roof above the school gym seemed useful only to pigeons. [2] Then environmental science teacher Marisol Vega proposed a small garden there, the principal was skeptical. [3] The roof, which had recently been reinforced could hold the added weight. [4] Students began by carrying up shallow planters, soil, and seedlings. [5] Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly. [6] The class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil. [7] These two changes reduced evaporation; as a result, the herbs survived the hottest weeks. [8] By September, the garden supplied basil and mint to the culinary arts class. [9] It also gave science students a place to measure how shade, wind, and soil depth affected plant growth.

**Question:** Which choice most effectively combines sentences 5 and 6?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly, the class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil. **(correct)**
- **b.** Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly. The class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil.
- **c.** Summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly, the class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch.
- **d.** Climbing quickly, the class installed a drip-irrigation line because of summer temperatures.

**Correct answer:** a — Because summer temperatures on the roof climbed quickly, the class installed a drip-irrigation line and spread pale mulch over the soil.

**Explanation:** The dependent because-clause must attach to the complete main clause with a comma.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-bus-1

**Stimulus:** [1] On winter evenings, graphic-design student Imani Cole waited for a bus that sometimes appeared early and sometimes not at all. [2] Rather than simply complain, she began recording each arrival in a small notebook. [3] After three weeks of collecting data Imani transferred the times to a digital map. [4] The map revealed that delays clustered near two busy intersections. [5] Imani shared her findings with the transit office, they asked her to continue the project. [6] She then invited other riders to submit observations through a simple form. [7] The expanded data did not eliminate delays; however, it helped dispatchers adjust the published schedule. [8] The project was completely unanimous among every rider who saw it. [9] Most important, the map made an unpredictable wait easier to plan around.

**Question:** Where should a comma be added in sentence 3?

**Choices:**

- **a.** After three weeks, of collecting data Imani transferred the times to a digital map.
- **b.** After three weeks of collecting data, Imani transferred the times to a digital map. **(correct)**
- **c.** After three weeks of collecting, data Imani transferred the times to a digital map.
- **d.** No comma is needed.

**Correct answer:** b — After three weeks of collecting data, Imani transferred the times to a digital map.

**Explanation:** The comma marks the end of the introductory prepositional phrase before the independent clause.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-bus-2

**Stimulus:** [1] On winter evenings, graphic-design student Imani Cole waited for a bus that sometimes appeared early and sometimes not at all. [2] Rather than simply complain, she began recording each arrival in a small notebook. [3] After three weeks of collecting data Imani transferred the times to a digital map. [4] The map revealed that delays clustered near two busy intersections. [5] Imani shared her findings with the transit office, they asked her to continue the project. [6] She then invited other riders to submit observations through a simple form. [7] The expanded data did not eliminate delays; however, it helped dispatchers adjust the published schedule. [8] The project was completely unanimous among every rider who saw it. [9] Most important, the map made an unpredictable wait easier to plan around.

**Question:** Which revision of sentence 5 is grammatically correct?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Imani shared her findings with the transit office, they asked her to continue the project.
- **b.** Imani shared her findings with the transit office; and they asked her to continue the project.
- **c.** Imani shared her findings with the transit office, and they asked her to continue the project. **(correct)**
- **d.** Sharing her findings with the transit office, they asked Imani to continue the project.

**Correct answer:** c — Imani shared her findings with the transit office, and they asked her to continue the project.

**Explanation:** A comma plus the coordinating conjunction “and” correctly joins the two independent clauses.

**Fixed from (rationale):** Comma plus the coordinating conjunction and correctly joins the two independent clauses.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-bus-4

**Stimulus:** [1] On winter evenings, graphic-design student Imani Cole waited for a bus that sometimes appeared early and sometimes not at all. ### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-clay-1

**Stimulus:** [1] Archaeologist Jun Park studied a palm-size clay object recovered near an ancient footpath. [2] The object had one opening at the top and two smaller holes along its side. [3] Park suspected it might be a whistle, but the original was too fragile to test. [4] Working from precise measurements, a ceramic artist created three replicas. [5] Each replica differed slightly in the angle of its internal chamber. [6] When air was blown across the replicas openings, only one produced a clear tone. [7] That result did not prove exactly how the object had been used. [8] Nevertheless it supported Park's claim that sound production was possible. [9] The experiment demonstrated the reason why replicas can help researchers test ideas while protecting delicate artifacts.

**Question:** Which choice most effectively describes the object without unnecessary wording?

**Choices:**

- **a.** a small object that was palm-size in its dimensions
- **b.** a palm-size clay object **(correct)**
- **c.** an object made of clay that was small-sized
- **d.** a clay object of a size that fit into a palm

**Correct answer:** b — a palm-size clay object

**Explanation:** Palm-size clay object conveys material and scale directly without repeating the idea of size.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-clay-4

**Stimulus:** [1] Archaeologist Jun Park studied a palm-size clay object recovered near an ancient footpath. [2] The object had one opening at the top and two smaller holes along its side. [3] Park suspected it might be a whistle, but the original was too fragile to test. [4] Working from precise measurements, a ceramic artist created three replicas. ### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-clay-5

**Stimulus:** [1] Archaeologist Jun Park studied a palm-size clay object recovered near an ancient footpath. [2] The object had one opening at the top and two smaller holes along its side. [3] Park suspected it might be a whistle, but the original was too fragile to test. [4] Working from precise measurements, a ceramic artist created three replicas. [5] Each replica differed slightly in the angle of its internal chamber. [6] When air was blown across the replicas openings, only one produced a clear tone. [7] That result did not prove exactly how the object had been used. [8] Nevertheless it supported Park's claim that sound production was possible. [9] The experiment demonstrated the reason why replicas can help researchers test ideas while protecting delicate artifacts.

**Question:** Which choice most effectively combines sentences 7 and 8?

**Choices:**

- **a.** That result did not prove exactly how the object had been used, nevertheless, it supported Park's claim.
- **b.** Although that result did not prove exactly how the object had been used, it supported Park's claim that sound production was possible. **(correct)**
- **c.** That result not proving exactly how the object had been used; supported Park's claim.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-stream-1

**Stimulus:** [1] Biologist Elena Ruiz records streams, not songs. [2] At each site, she lowers a waterproof microphone into the current for exactly ten minutes. [3] The recordings capture insect clicks, fish movement, rainfall, and the low rumble of stones shifting underwater. [4] Ruiz labels every file with its location date and water temperature. [5] Over time, her audio library has revealed seasonal patterns that are difficult to observe during a single visit. [6] For example, one insect species becomes audible several days before it appears in standard net samples. [7] The recordings are not a replacement for field measurements they are another source of evidence. [8] Ruiz plans to make the collection publicly accessible, allowing students to compare waterways from different regions. [9] In this way, listeners can hear an ecosystem changing before the change becomes visible.

**Question:** Which revision of sentence 4 uses commas correctly?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Ruiz labels every file with its location, date, and water temperature. **(correct)**
- **b.** Ruiz labels every file with, its location date and water temperature.
- **c.** Ruiz labels every file with its location date, and water temperature.
- **d.** Ruiz labels every file, with its location, date and water temperature.

**Correct answer:** a — Ruiz labels every file with its location, date, and water temperature.

**Explanation:** The three label fields form a series and should be separated consistently with commas.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-repair-1

**Stimulus:** [1] Every second Saturday, the back room of the neighborhood library becomes a repair café. [2] Residents bring lamps, clocks, and small appliances that would otherwise be discarded. [3] Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed, instead they explain each diagnosis as they work. [4] A teenager who arrived with a silent desk fan learned to test its switch and replace a damaged wire. [5] The fan began spinning again. [6] More importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped. [7] The café's goal is not merely just to save broken objects. [8] It also makes repair knowledge less mysterious and gives neighbors a reason to exchange skills. [9] Since opening last year the café has hosted forty sessions and diverted hundreds of items from the landfill.

**Question:** Which revision of sentence 3 is grammatically correct?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed, instead they explain each diagnosis.
- **b.** Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed; instead, they explain each diagnosis as they work. **(correct)**
- **c.** Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed instead, explaining each diagnosis.
- **d.** Volunteers do not promise, that every object can be fixed; instead they explain each diagnosis.

**Correct answer:** b — Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed; instead, they explain each diagnosis as they work.

**Explanation:** The semicolon separates independent clauses, and commas correctly set off the introductory adverb instead.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-repair-4

**Stimulus:** [1] Every second Saturday, the back room of the neighborhood library becomes a repair café. [2] Residents bring lamps, clocks, and small appliances that would otherwise be discarded. [3] Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed, instead they explain each diagnosis as they work. [4] A teenager who arrived with a silent desk fan learned to test its switch and replace a damaged wire. [5] The fan began spinning again. [6] More importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped. [7] The café's goal is not merely just to save broken objects. [8] It also makes repair knowledge less mysterious and gives neighbors a reason to exchange skills. [9] Since opening last year the café has hosted forty sessions and diverted hundreds of items from the landfill.

**Question:** Where should a comma be added in sentence 9?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Since opening, last year the café has hosted forty sessions
- **b.** Since opening last year, the café has hosted forty sessions **(correct)**
- **c.** Since opening last year the café, has hosted forty sessions
- **d.** No comma is needed

**Correct answer:** b — Since opening last year, the café has hosted forty sessions

**Explanation:** The comma marks the end of the introductory phrase before the main clause.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · eng-repair-5

**Stimulus:** [1] Every second Saturday, the back room of the neighborhood library becomes a repair café. [2] Residents bring lamps, clocks, and small appliances that would otherwise be discarded. [3] Volunteers do not promise that every object can be fixed, instead they explain each diagnosis as they work. [4] A teenager who arrived with a silent desk fan learned to test its switch and replace a damaged wire. [5] The fan began spinning again. [6] More importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped. [7] The café's goal is not merely just to save broken objects. [8] It also makes repair knowledge less mysterious and gives neighbors a reason to exchange skills. [9] Since opening last year the café has hosted forty sessions and diverted hundreds of items from the landfill.

**Question:** Which choice most effectively combines sentences 5 and 6?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The fan began spinning again, more importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped.
- **b.** The fan began spinning again; more importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped. **(correct)**
- **c.** The fan beginning to spin again, and more importantly the teenager understanding why.
- **d.** More importantly the fan began spinning again and the teenager left.

**Correct answer:** b — The fan began spinning again; more importantly, the teenager left understanding why it had stopped.

**Explanation:** A semicolon joins the two complete sentences, and a comma follows the transitional phrase more importantly.

- **A.** Complete sentence
- **B.** Run-on sentence
- **C.** Fragment **(correct)**
- **D.** Correctly joined clauses

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · sentence-boundaries-practice-5

**Stimulus:** The observatory's oldest telescope still produces remarkably sharp images. Its original tracking motor, however, turns unevenly after decades of use, the staff therefore guides the telescope by hand during long exposures.

**Question:** Which revision of the final sentence most effectively corrects the sentence boundary while preserving its logical relationship?

**Choices:**

- **A.** Its original tracking motor, however, turns unevenly after decades of use, the staff therefore guides the telescope by hand during long exposures.
- **B.** Its original tracking motor, however, turns unevenly after decades of use; therefore, the staff guides the telescope by hand during long exposures. **(correct)**
- **C.** Its original tracking motor, however, turning unevenly after decades of use, therefore the staff guides the telescope by hand during long exposures.
- **D.** Its original tracking motor, however, turns unevenly after decades of use and therefore, guiding the telescope by hand during long exposures.

**Correct answer:** B — Its original tracking motor, however, turns unevenly after decades of use; therefore, the staff guides the telescope by hand during long exposures.

**Explanation:** Both main ideas are independent clauses. A semicolon correctly joins them, and therefore is set off with a following comma to show that the staff's action results from the motor's uneven motion.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** A comma alone cannot join the two independent clauses.
- **C:** Turning removes the working verb from the first clause and leaves the structure incomplete.
- **D:** The revision makes guiding a modifier without a clear grammatical subject.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · concision-and-redundancy-practice-5

**Stimulus:** Because the archive's paper maps are fragile, volunteers first create digital scans. The scans provide a substitute copy that can be used in place of the original when researchers need to compare handwritten notes.

**Question:** Which revision of the second sentence is most concise while preserving all of its relevant meaning?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The scans provide a substitute copy that can be used in place of the original when researchers need to compare handwritten notes.
- **B.** The scans can be used instead of the original maps when researchers compare handwritten notes. **(correct)**
- **C.** The scans, which are digital in their format, are substitute copies for researchers and their handwritten-note comparisons.
- **D.** Researchers compare handwritten notes, and the scans are used.

**Correct answer:** B — The scans can be used instead of the original maps when researchers compare handwritten notes.

**Explanation:** Choice B keeps the purpose of the scans, the comparison task, and the contrast with the original maps while removing the repeated ideas in substitute copy and used in place of.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** Substitute and used in place of repeat the same relationship.
- **C:** The revision adds wordy nominalizations and repeats information from the first sentence.
- **D:** The revision loses the important relationship between the scans and the fragile originals.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · punctuation-and-commas-practice-1

**Stimulus:** The chef, who trained in Lima, opened a cafe.

**Question:** Which choice uses punctuation correctly?

**Choices:**

- **A.** Remove the first comma only
- **B.** Keep both commas **(correct)**
- **C.** Remove the second comma only
- **D.** Remove both commas

**Correct answer:** B — Keep both commas

**Explanation:** The middle clause is nonessential and needs paired commas.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.
- **C:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.
- **D:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.

- **B.** Remove the comma
- **C.** Replace the comma with a semicolon and keep and
- **D.** Add a second comma after hikers

**Correct answer:** A — Keep the comma

**Explanation:** A comma plus the coordinating conjunction “and” correctly joins two complete sentences.

**Choice explanations:**

- **B:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.
- **C:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.
- **D:** Name the grammatical role of the phrase or clause before choosing punctuation; do not punctuate by pause.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · punctuation-and-commas-practice-5

**Stimulus:** The cedar waxwing, unlike many birds that defend a feeding territory will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby.

**Question:** Which choice uses commas correctly?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The cedar waxwing unlike many birds that defend a feeding territory, will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby.
- **B.** The cedar waxwing, unlike many birds that defend a feeding territory, will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby. **(correct)**
- **C.** The cedar waxwing, unlike many birds, that defend a feeding territory will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby.
- **D.** The cedar waxwing unlike many birds, that defend a feeding territory, will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby.

**Correct answer:** B — The cedar waxwing, unlike many birds that defend a feeding territory, will often pass berries to another waxwing perched nearby.

**Explanation:** Unlike many birds that defend a feeding territory is a removable contrast that interrupts the main clause and therefore needs commas at both ends. Within that phrase, that defend a feeding territory identifies which birds and should not be separated.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The opening edge of the interrupting contrast is not marked.
- **C:** The comma wrongly separates “birds” from the restrictive relative clause that modifies the noun.
- **D:** The revision both misses the start of the interruption and separates a restrictive clause.

**Fixed from (choice C explanation):** The comma wrongly separates birds from its restrictive relative clause.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · logical-transitions-practice-1

- **D.** Similarly

**Correct answer:** B — However

**Explanation:** The second sentence contrasts price advantage with poor durability.

- **A:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **C:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **D:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.

- **D.** For instance

- **B:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **C:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **D:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.

- **B.** Consequently

**Explanation:** The second action adds another measurement.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **B:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.
- **C:** This transition signals a different relationship from the one created by the surrounding sentences.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · logical-transitions-practice-4

**Stimulus:** The route was longer. ___, it avoided the flooded bridge.

- **B.** For example
- **C.** Still **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** C — Still

**Explanation:** The second idea explains why the longer route remained useful.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · logical-transitions-practice-5

**Stimulus:** Early acoustic panels absorbed a broad range of frequencies but also muffled speech. Newer panels can be tuned to absorb only the frequencies most likely to produce echoes. ______, a concert hall can reduce distracting reverberation without making a speaker's voice sound dull.

**Question:** Which transition most logically completes the final sentence?

**Choices:**

- **A.** For instance,
- **B.** Nevertheless,
- **C.** As a result, **(correct)**
- **D.** In comparison,

**Correct answer:** C — As a result,

**Explanation:** The final sentence gives the practical result of tuning panels to a narrower range of frequencies. As a result accurately labels that cause-and-effect relationship.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The final sentence states a consequence, not one example of frequency tuning.
- **B:** The final sentence does not contrast with the benefit described before it.
- **D:** The final sentence explains an outcome rather than setting two halls side by side.

**Correct answer:** b — The telescope image blurred after a week of outdoor testing; therefore, the observer adjusted the tracking motor.

**Explanation:** The ideas on both sides are independent clauses. A semicolon separates them, and the conjunctive adverb therefore takes a following comma.

## Math

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · math-01

**Question:** What value of x satisfies 5(2x − 3) = 4x + 21?

**Choices:**

- **a.** 3
- **b.** 4
- **c.** 5
- **d.** 6 **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — 6

**Explanation:** Expanding gives 10x − 15 = 4x + 21. Subtract 4x and add 15 to get 6x = 36, so x = 6.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · math-02

**Question:** For f(x) = x² − 4x + 7, define g(x) = f(x + 2). Which expression is equivalent to g(x)?

**Choices:**

- **a.** x² − 8x + 19
- **b.** x² − 4x + 3
- **c.** x² + 3 **(correct)**
- **d.** x² + 4x + 7

**Correct answer:** c — x² + 3

**Explanation:** Replace each x in f with x + 2: (x + 2)² − 4(x + 2) + 7. Expanding and combining like terms gives x² + 3.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · functions-and-modeling-practice-5

**Question:** A repair service charges $48 for the first 2 hours of work and $12 for each additional hour. For h > 2, which expression gives the total charge C(h), in dollars, for h hours of work?

**Choices:**

- **A.** C(h) = 48 + 12h
- **B.** C(h) = 48 + 12(h − 2) **(correct)**
- **C.** C(h) = 48h + 12
- **D.** C(h) = 24h + 12(h − 2)

**Correct answer:** B — C(h) = 48 + 12(h − 2)

**Explanation:** The first 2 hours cost a fixed $48. Only h − 2 hours remain after those first 2 hours, so the additional charge is 12(h − 2), making the total 48 + 12(h − 2).

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This charges the additional-hour rate for the first 2 hours as well.
- **C:** This treats the fixed first-2-hour charge as an hourly rate.
- **D:** This invents a per-hour breakdown for the fixed charge and adds it again.

- **c.** f⁻¹(x) = 3x + 4 **(correct)**
- **d.** f⁻¹(x) = 1/(3x − 4)

**Correct answer:** c — f⁻¹(x) = 3x + 4

**Explanation:** Swap x and y, then solve for y; multiplication by the denominator and addition of the constant undo the original operations.

## Reading

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · read-watch-1

**Stimulus:** When Leena inherited her grandfather's workbench, she expected the obvious tools: magnifying lenses, narrow screwdrivers, tweezers fine enough to lift a grain of rice. What surprised her was the shallow bottom drawer. It held dozens of watch parts that appeared useless—clouded faces, bent hands, springs no wider than thread. Each rested in a paper envelope marked only with a date. Leena nearly emptied the drawer into a recycling bin. Instead, she opened one envelope and found a note on the back: 'Running fast in cold weather.' Another read, 'Stops when carried uphill.' The drawer was not a collection of spare parts. It was a record of failures. Her grandfather had kept the damaged pieces because each preserved a problem that a polished repair would hide. Over the next month, Leena arranged the envelopes by symptom and compared them with his repair logs. Patterns emerged. Several watches that lost time in winter used the same thin lubricant; watches damaged on steep hikes often had loose balance springs. The discarded parts became an index to decisions her grandfather had made years earlier. Leena still could not ask him why he had chosen one repair over another. Yet by studying what he refused to throw away, she began to hear an answer in the evidence he had left behind.

**Question:** Which statement best expresses the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Leena learns that damaged watch parts preserve evidence about her grandfather's repair decisions. **(correct)**
- **b.** Leena discovers that old watches are more valuable than modern watches.
- **c.** Leena decides to become a professional watchmaker after finding expensive tools.
- **d.** Leena proves that her grandfather used the wrong lubricant in every repair.

**Correct answer:** a — Leena learns that damaged watch parts preserve evidence about her grandfather's repair decisions.

**Explanation:** The passage centers on Leena recognizing the drawer as a record of problems and decisions rather than useless parts.

The discarded parts became an index to decisions her grandfather had made years earlier. Leena still could not ask him why he had chosen one repair over another. **Question:** Which detail first causes Leena to reconsider discarding the contents of the drawer?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The presence of magnifying lenses
- **b.** The dates written on the envelopes
- **c.** A note describing a watch running fast in cold weather **(correct)**
- **d.** The discovery of loose balance springs

**Correct answer:** c — A note describing a watch running fast in cold weather

**Explanation:** Leena changes course immediately after opening an envelope and reading the symptom written on it.

**Question:** It can reasonably be inferred that Leena's grandfather kept the damaged parts primarily because they:

**Choices:**

- **a.** might eventually become valuable collectibles
- **b.** could document the causes of recurring watch problems **(correct)**
- **c.** were too small to dispose of safely
- **d.** belonged to customers who might return for them

**Correct answer:** b — could document the causes of recurring watch problems

**Explanation:** The symptom notes, repair logs, and patterns show that the parts functioned as a record of recurring problems.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · read-watch-4

**Stimulus:** When Leena inherited her grandfather's workbench, she expected the obvious tools: magnifying lenses, narrow screwdrivers, tweezers fine enough to lift a grain of rice. What surprised her was the shallow bottom drawer. It held dozens of watch parts that appeared useless—clouded faces, bent hands, springs no wider than thread. Each rested in a paper envelope marked only with a date. Leena nearly emptied the drawer into a recycling bin. Instead, she opened one envelope and found a note on the back: 'Running fast in cold weather.' Another read, 'Stops when carried uphill.' The drawer was not a collection of spare parts. It was a record of failures. Her grandfather had kept the damaged pieces because each preserved a problem that a polished repair would hide. Over the next month, Leena arranged the envelopes by symptom and compared them with his repair logs. Patterns emerged. Several watches that lost time in winter used the same thin lubricant; watches damaged on steep hikes often had loose balance springs. The discarded parts became an index to decisions her grandfather had made years earlier. Leena still could not ask him why he had chosen one repair over another. Yet by studying what he refused to throw away, she began to hear an answer in the evidence he had left behind.

**Question:** The final sentence primarily serves to:

**Choices:**

- **a.** claim that Leena can literally hear her grandfather speaking
- **b.** shift the passage from evidence-based discovery to unsupported fantasy
- **c.** emphasize that material evidence allows Leena to understand decisions he can no longer explain **(correct)**
- **d.** show that Leena has completed every repair in the logs

**Correct answer:** c — emphasize that material evidence allows Leena to understand decisions he can no longer explain

**Explanation:** The figurative phrasing connects Leena's emotional loss with the concrete evidence she studies.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · read-city-1

**Stimulus:** Cities often estimate summer heat using satellite images, which reveal broad patterns across roofs, roads, and parks. Those images are useful, but they can miss the temperature differences a pedestrian experiences within a single block. To capture that smaller scale, a research team in Mesa Verde equipped bicycles with temperature and humidity sensors. Volunteers rode assigned routes at the same times on cloudless afternoons. The resulting maps showed sharp changes: a shaded sidewalk could be several degrees cooler than an exposed crossing less than fifty meters away. The team then compared these measurements with tree-canopy maps and building heights. Mature trees generally produced the largest cooling effect, but narrow streets shaded by buildings also stayed relatively cool. Importantly, the researchers did not treat every cool location as proof that a tree should be planted there. Underground utilities, water access, and pedestrian traffic affect whether a planting is practical. Instead, the block-level data helped planners identify places where shade was both needed and feasible. Satellite images still supplied the regional view; bicycle measurements supplied the street-level detail. Used together, the methods turned a general statement—some neighborhoods are hotter—into a set of specific design decisions.

**Question:** The passage's main purpose is to explain how:

**Choices:**

- **a.** satellite images should be replaced by bicycle measurements
- **b.** street-level data can refine broad heat maps into practical shade decisions **(correct)**
- **c.** volunteers can ride bicycles safely in hot weather
- **d.** building shade is always more effective than tree shade

**Correct answer:** b — street-level data can refine broad heat maps into practical shade decisions

**Explanation:** The passage emphasizes combining regional and block-level data to guide feasible interventions.

### Round 0 diagnostic · AlexACT full 66-question diagnostic · read-translate-1

**Stimulus:** Translator A: When I translate a poem, I begin with its movement. I read it aloud until I can feel where the language accelerates, hesitates, or turns. A literal version may preserve dictionary meanings while losing the experience of the original. If a line in the source language arrives like a sudden knock, I want the English line to interrupt the reader in a similar way—even if that requires changing word order. Fidelity, to me, includes the reader's physical encounter with the poem.<br><br>Translator B: I also read aloud, but I begin by mapping the poem's repeated images and key terms. A surprising word should not become ordinary merely because an ordinary word sounds smoother in English. Repetition may feel awkward, yet that awkwardness can be deliberate. I am willing to alter syntax, but I resist replacing a concrete image with a general feeling. The translated poem should not imitate English habits so comfortably that the original poem's strangeness disappears.<br><br>Both translators reject word-for-word substitution as a complete method. Their disagreement is one of emphasis. Translator A is most willing to reshape the sentence to recreate pace and impact. Translator B places a firmer boundary around recurring words and images. Each treats form as part of meaning; each simply listens for a different part of the form first.

**Question:** Both translators agree that:

**Choices:**

- **a.** English word order should never change
- **b.** reading aloud has no role in translation
- **c.** word-for-word substitution is insufficient **(correct)**
- **d.** recurring images should always be replaced

**Correct answer:** c — word-for-word substitution is insufficient

**Explanation:** The final paragraph explicitly states that both reject word-for-word substitution as a complete method.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · central-ideas-and-details-practice-1

**Question:** A passage traces a scientist's repeated tests and revisions. What is the central idea?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The scientist preferred the first test
- **B.** Progress came through revision **(correct)**
- **C.** The laboratory equipment was expensive
- **D.** One revision changed a measurement

**Correct answer:** B — Progress came through revision

**Explanation:** The repeated testing pattern controls the passage.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This choice is too narrow, unsupported, or misses the pattern controlling the whole passage.
- **C:** This choice is too narrow, unsupported, or misses the pattern controlling the whole passage.
- **D:** This choice is too narrow, unsupported, or misses the pattern controlling the whole passage.

- **D:** This choice is too narrow, unsupported, or misses the pattern controlling the whole passage.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · central-ideas-and-details-practice-5

**Stimulus:** For decades, botanists classified the silverleaf vine as a single species because plants across its range produced nearly identical flowers. Ecologist Mina Rao noticed, however, that northern populations released their pollen weeks earlier than southern populations. Genetic analysis later revealed two distinct lineages. The flowers had hidden a difference that timing and DNA made visible.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **A.** Flower shape is usually unreliable when botanists classify plants.
- **B.** Evidence from reproductive timing and genetics showed that one apparent vine species contained two lineages. **(correct)**
- **C.** Northern silverleaf vines release more pollen than southern vines.
- **D.** Rao's genetic methods were more accurate than every earlier botanical method.

**Correct answer:** B — Evidence from reproductive timing and genetics showed that one apparent vine species contained two lineages.

**Explanation:** Choice B combines the original classification, Rao's timing observation, and the later genetic evidence. The other choices either add unsupported general claims or focus on a detail the passage does not state.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The passage makes a narrower claim about one vine, not a general rule about flower shape.
- **C:** The passage compares timing, not the amount of pollen.
- **D:** The passage does not rank all methods or attribute the genetic analysis solely to Rao.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · supported-inference-practice-1

**Question:** A character saves receipts and checks prices twice. What is supported?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The character never buys anything
- **B.** The character is careful with spending **(correct)**
- **C.** The character works as an accountant
- **D.** The receipts are required by law

**Correct answer:** B — The character is careful with spending

**Explanation:** The details support caution about money.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.
- **C:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.
- **D:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · supported-inference-practice-2

**Question:** A researcher repeats a trial after a surprising result. What is supported?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The researcher wants more reliable evidence **(correct)**
- **B.** The researcher expected the result to fail
- **C.** The first trial was definitely incorrect
- **D.** The researcher wants a different outcome

**Correct answer:** A — The researcher wants more reliable evidence

**Explanation:** Repeating a trial strengthens confidence.

**Choice explanations:**

- **B:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.
- **C:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.
- **D:** This choice adds a motive, certainty, or fact that the supplied evidence does not establish.

- **D.** The narrator expects bad weather may affect travel **(correct)**

**Question:** A student whispers before entering the auditorium. What is supported?

- **B.** The auditorium is empty
- **C.** The setting likely requires quiet **(correct)**
- **D.** The student has lost their voice

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · supported-inference-practice-5

**Stimulus:** At the first rehearsal, conductor Lena Ortiz asked the string players to shorten several sustained notes. During the next run, she stood at the back of the empty hall rather than at the podium. After listening there, Ortiz restored the notes to their original lengths but asked the brass section to play more softly beneath them.

**Question:** Which inference about Ortiz is best supported by the passage?

**Choices:**

- **A.** She believed the string players had ignored her first instruction.
- **B.** She changed her diagnosis of the balance problem after listening from a different location. **(correct)**
- **C.** She preferred rehearsing without any brass players present.
- **D.** She intended the next performance to take place in an empty hall.

**Correct answer:** B — She changed her diagnosis of the balance problem after listening from a different location.

**Explanation:** Ortiz first changes the string notes, then listens from the back of the hall, reverses that change, and adjusts the brass instead. That sequence supports a revised diagnosis, not player disobedience or a preference for an empty hall.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The passage gives no evidence that the players failed to follow directions.
- **C:** She asks the brass to adjust; she does not ask them to leave.
- **D:** The empty hall is the rehearsal setting, not a stated performance plan.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · textual-evidence-and-details-practice-1

**Question:** The passage says the bridge opened in 1920. Which answer is supported?

- **A.** The bridge was built in exactly one year
- **B.** The bridge opened in 1920 **(correct)**
- **C.** The bridge closed in 1920
- **D.** The bridge was the first in the region

**Correct answer:** B — The bridge opened in 1920

**Explanation:** This restates the explicit date detail.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The passage states a different detail; return to the exact wording before paraphrasing it.
- **C:** The passage states a different detail; return to the exact wording before paraphrasing it.
- **D:** The passage states a different detail; return to the exact wording before paraphrasing it.

**Fixed from (choice B):** The bridge was open by 1920

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · textual-evidence-and-details-practice-5

**Stimulus:** Engineer Sol Kim tested two coatings on identical roof tiles. Coating P reflected more sunlight at noon, but its performance dropped sharply after dust settled on it. Coating Q reflected slightly less sunlight when clean yet retained most of that ability after a month outdoors. Kim recommended Q for buildings in dry regions where rain would rarely wash the tiles.

**Question:** Which detail from the passage most directly supports Kim's recommendation?

**Choices:**

- **A.** The coatings were tested on identical roof tiles.
- **B.** Coating P reflected more sunlight at noon when it was clean.
- **C.** Coating Q retained most of its reflective ability after a month outdoors. **(correct)**
- **D.** Rain would rarely wash roof tiles in the recommended regions.

**Correct answer:** C — Coating Q retained most of its reflective ability after a month outdoors.

**Explanation:** Kim recommends Q because it continues to perform after outdoor exposure and dust accumulation. Choice D explains why durability matters, but choice C is the result that directly establishes Q's advantage under that condition.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This makes the comparison fair but does not favor Q for dry regions.
- **B:** This detail favors P only under the condition that did not drive the recommendation.
- **D:** This identifies the regional condition but needs the durability result to support choosing Q.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · author-purpose-and-structure-practice-1

**Question:** An author gives a personal anecdote before statistics. What is the likely purpose?

**Choices:**

- **A.** To replace the statistics with opinion
- **B.** To introduce the issue through an example **(correct)**
- **C.** To prove the issue affects only one person
- **D.** To reveal the author's final conclusion

**Correct answer:** B — To introduce the issue through an example

**Explanation:** The anecdote makes the issue concrete before data appears.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **C:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **D:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · author-purpose-and-structure-practice-2

**Question:** A paragraph begins with critics' objections, then answers them. What is its job?

**Choices:**

- **A.** Address counterarguments **(correct)**
- **B.** Introduce an unrelated historical detail
- **C.** Repeat the critics' claims as facts
- **D.** Avoid taking a position

**Correct answer:** A — Address counterarguments

**Explanation:** It presents objections so they can be answered.

**Choice explanations:**

- **B:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **C:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **D:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.

**Question:** Why include a definition before a technical explanation?

**Choices:**

- **A.** To make the explanation more technical
- **B.** To delay the passage's main idea
- **C.** To contradict the later explanation
- **D.** To clarify a key term **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** D — To clarify a key term

**Explanation:** The definition prepares readers for the explanation.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **B:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **C:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · author-purpose-and-structure-practice-4

**Question:** A final paragraph returns to the opening image. What is the structural purpose?

**Choices:**

- **A.** Introduce a completely new argument
- **B.** Show that the opening image was inaccurate
- **C.** Create a closing connection **(correct)**
- **D.** Summarize every detail in order

**Correct answer:** C — Create a closing connection

**Explanation:** Returning to an opening image frames the ending.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **B:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.
- **D:** This choice does not describe the actual job the referenced part performs in the passage.

### Lesson practice · Lesson practice · author-purpose-and-structure-practice-5

**Stimulus:** When a city replaces asphalt with pale pavement, daytime surface temperatures often fall. That result seems to make pale pavement an obvious response to summer heat. Yet some researchers caution that the brighter surface can reflect additional sunlight toward pedestrians and nearby buildings. They argue that cities should measure air temperature and human heat exposure, not surface temperature alone, before choosing where to use the material.

**Question:** The third sentence primarily serves to:

**Choices:**

- **A.** reject the claim that pale pavement lowers surface temperatures.
- **B.** introduce a limitation that complicates the apparent benefit described earlier. **(correct)**
- **C.** provide a historical explanation for why cities first used asphalt.
- **D.** summarize the measurements researchers recommend in the final sentence.

**Correct answer:** B — introduce a limitation that complicates the apparent benefit described earlier.

**Explanation:** The third sentence begins with Yet and introduces reflected sunlight as a drawback that complicates the preceding benefit. It does not deny the temperature result; it qualifies what that result means for people and buildings.

**Choice explanations:**

- **A:** The sentence qualifies the benefit rather than denying the earlier result.
- **C:** The passage gives no history of asphalt use.
- **D:** The measurement recommendation follows from the limitation; the third sentence does not summarize it.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form cedar · cedar-reading-01

**Stimulus:** Photographer Eli Moreno left hundreds of mountain images labeled only by month. Geologist Rina Cho initially doubted they could support research because the camera positions were unknown. Then she noticed recurring fence posts and cliff notches in the frames. By matching those fixed points with modern coordinates, Cho reconstructed twelve viewpoints. The photographs revealed that one glacier retreated unevenly: its shaded edge barely moved while the sun-exposed edge pulled back rapidly. The collection was not a planned scientific survey, yet its repeated visual anchors made careful comparison possible. Cho published both the measurements and the uncertainty remaining at each viewpoint.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Moreno planned a complete glacier survey.
- **b.** Every glacier edge retreated at the same rate.
- **c.** Cho converts an informal photograph collection into qualified evidence of uneven glacier retreat. **(correct)**
- **d.** Unknown camera positions make old photographs useless.

**Correct answer:** c — Cho converts an informal photograph collection into qualified evidence of uneven glacier retreat.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form cedar · cedar-reading-07

**Stimulus:** A city noise map averaged sound across large zones and classified Alder Street as moderate. Residents, however, complained about brief nighttime bursts from delivery carts. Researchers placed small monitors at doorways and recorded both volume and duration. The street was quiet most of the hour, but metal wheels crossing one drain cover produced repeated peaks loud enough to wake sleepers. Repaving the entire block would have been expensive. Replacing the drain cover removed most peaks while barely changing the hourly average. The fine-grained record identified a narrow cause hidden inside a broad summary.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Detailed nighttime measurements reveal and solve a brief noise problem that a zone-wide average concealed. **(correct)**
- **b.** Hourly averages always exaggerate street noise.
- **c.** The city repaves every block near Alder Street.
- **d.** Residents complain because the street is continuously loud.

**Correct answer:** a — Detailed nighttime measurements reveal and solve a brief noise problem that a zone-wide average concealed.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form cedar · cedar-reading-13

**Stimulus:** Historian A: Reenacting a workshop process can reveal practical limits a written instruction omits. When a recipe says to heat glue 'until ready,' repeated trials can show the workable temperature range.<br><br>Historian B: Trials are valuable, but modern materials and expectations can quietly shape the result. I treat reenactment as a question-generating tool, not a direct replay of the past.<br><br>Both historians use physical experiments. A is more willing to draw conclusions about technique from a successful reconstruction; B insists that each conclusion remain tied to differences between the historical and modern conditions.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Historian B refuses to conduct physical experiments.
- **b.** Historian A believes written sources contain every detail.
- **c.** Both historians value reenactment, but they differ in how directly its results can support claims about past practice. **(correct)**
- **d.** Both historians regard modern materials as identical to historical ones.

**Correct answer:** c — Both historians value reenactment, but they differ in how directly its results can support claims about past practice.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

**Question:** Which new finding would most strongly support the passage's central interpretation?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Some workshops have large windows.
- **b.** Historical recipes use old spelling.
- **c.** Glue containers come in many sizes.
- **d.** Reconstructions using different plausible modern materials produce different results from the same written instruction. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — Reconstructions using different plausible modern materials produce different results from the same written instruction.

**Explanation:** The correct finding directly tests and reinforces the passage's explanation; the other facts may be true but do not bear on that explanation.

**Stimulus:** A village archive listed payments to a bell founder but never named the workshop's location. Researcher Malik Grant plotted charcoal deliveries, rent records, and complaints about nighttime hammering. The three records used different street names because the district had been renumbered. Grant matched buildings through neighboring owners rather than numbers alone. All three trails pointed to a courtyard behind the former grain market. Soil samples there contained copper droplets and fragments of casting molds. The workshop's location became convincing because documents and material traces supported the same place.

- **a.** Grant identifies a lost bell workshop by reconciling changing addresses and matching documents with physical remains. **(correct)**
- **b.** A payment record states the workshop's exact address.
- **c.** Street numbers in the district never changed.
- **d.** Copper droplets prove every courtyard held a foundry.

**Correct answer:** a — Grant identifies a lost bell workshop by reconciling changing addresses and matching documents with physical remains.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form cedar · cedar-reading-25

**Stimulus:** Coastal managers planted silver cordgrass to hold sand around a restored dune. Its dense roots did stabilize the slope. They also trapped so much sand that the low openings used by nesting shorebirds gradually closed. Managers first considered removing the grass entirely, but exposed sections eroded during storms. They instead cut narrow corridors before nesting season and monitored both dune height and bird movement. The revised plan preserved much of the grass's protective effect while restoring access. Measuring only erosion would have labeled the first design a success; measuring habitat use revealed the tradeoff.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Cordgrass fails to stabilize any part of the dune.
- **b.** Shorebirds nest only in dense grass.
- **c.** Managers revise a successful erosion-control planting after broader monitoring reveals harm to shorebird access. **(correct)**
- **d.** Managers remove every plant before storms.

**Correct answer:** c — Managers revise a successful erosion-control planting after broader monitoring reveals harm to shorebird access.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form cedar · cedar-reading-31

**Stimulus:** Painter Laila Chen mixed four versions of the same blue pigment with oils that dried at different rates. She brushed each mixture across a tilted panel, then returned at fixed intervals to drag a comb through the paint. Fast-drying mixtures preserved sharp ridges; slow ones settled into soft bands. Chen did not choose one mixture as best. She layered them, placing crisp marks over broad fields that continued to shift beneath them. The drying rate became part of the composition's timing. It limited when a mark could be changed, yet those limits helped Chen coordinate textures she could not produce all at once.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Chen uses different drying rates as time-based constraints for coordinating layered textures. **(correct)**
- **b.** Chen searches for one oil that dries faster than all others.
- **c.** Every mixture produces the same texture.
- **d.** Chen avoids changing paint after it reaches the panel.

**Correct answer:** a — Chen uses different drying rates as time-based constraints for coordinating layered textures.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form delta · delta-reading-01

**Stimulus:** Mina Patel found a cabinet of old seed-exchange cards whose formal descriptions were brief, but growers had crowded the backs with notes: 'bolted after three hot nights,' 'sweetest from the shaded row,' and 'survived the late frost.' Patel matched the cards with weather records and field maps. Varieties praised in one decade sometimes failed in another because planting dates had shifted. The cards were not controlled experiments, yet together they preserved many small comparisons across places and years. Patel used them to choose candidates for new trials rather than to declare one variety universally best.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Patel proves one seed variety is best in every climate.
- **b.** The cards contain complete controlled experiments.
- **c.** Weather records show planting dates never changed.
- **d.** Patel uses informal grower observations as leads for new seed trials while respecting their limits. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — Patel uses informal grower observations as leads for new seed trials while respecting their limits.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form delta · delta-reading-07

**Stimulus:** Regional tree-canopy maps classified Juniper Plaza as well shaded, but people still avoided its benches at midday. Landscape researchers mounted light sensors at seat height and recorded shade minute by minute. Tall trees covered most of the plaza area, yet wind moved their narrow crowns enough to expose the benches repeatedly. A nearby wall cast a smaller but steadier shadow. The team shifted two benches into that stable band and planted lower shrubs near the others. Area alone had overstated the comfort provided by moving shade. Duration at the exact place people sat proved more useful.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Juniper Plaza contains no trees.
- **b.** Minute-by-minute measurements at bench height reveal why broad canopy coverage did not provide reliable comfort. **(correct)**
- **c.** Walls always produce more shade area than trees.
- **d.** Researchers remove every bench from the plaza.

**Correct answer:** b — Minute-by-minute measurements at bench height reveal why broad canopy coverage did not provide reliable comfort.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

**Stimulus:** Regional tree-canopy maps classified Juniper Plaza as well shaded, but people still avoided its benches at midday. Duration at the exact place people sat proved more useful.

- **d.** a place where wind speed increased

**Correct answer:** b — an area that stayed shaded consistently

**Stimulus:** Curator A: When a sculpture lacks an arm, I avoid adding a full replacement unless its shape is documented. I use a neutral color and label the evidence behind the proposed shape.<br><br>Both curators reject an unmarked imitation. A gives greater weight to leaving uncertainty physically open; B is more willing to visualize a supported hypothesis as long as viewers can distinguish it from original material.

- **a.** Both curators favor invisible imitations.
- **b.** Curator A believes damaged sculptures should never be displayed.
- **c.** Curator B ignores evidence about original poses.
- **d.** The curators agree additions must be distinguishable but differ over whether a supported reconstruction should fill a missing form. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — The curators agree additions must be distinguishable but differ over whether a supported reconstruction should fill a missing form.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form delta · delta-reading-19

**Stimulus:** A mining town's winter road vanished after the river shifted course. Historian Tess Okafor compared sled-freight receipts, diary references to travel time, and rows of cut stumps visible in aerial images. Receipts named destinations but not the route. Diaries described steep climbs. The stump line marked where crews had once cleared a corridor. Okafor modeled several possible paths and walked each after snowfall. Only one matched both the recorded travel times and the documented climb. Rusted horseshoe nails found along that corridor added material support to the reconstruction.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** A receipt prints the road's complete route.
- **b.** Okafor reconstructs a lost winter road by testing a route against documentary, landscape, and material evidence. **(correct)**
- **c.** Every possible path matches the diary times.
- **d.** The river's old course is still visible.

**Correct answer:** b — Okafor reconstructs a lost winter road by testing a route against documentary, landscape, and material evidence.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form delta · delta-reading-25

**Stimulus:** Managers added filter-feeding oysters to a coastal pond to reduce suspended algae. Water clarity improved quickly, allowing sunlight to reach deeper sediment. That change encouraged thick mats of bottom algae, which trapped gas and lifted loose sediment when they broke apart. The pond became clear in the water column but unstable at the bottom. Managers reduced oyster density and added periodic sediment checks. The intervention had met its original clarity target while exposing a second process the target did not measure.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Oysters fail to filter any suspended algae.
- **b.** Clear water prevents sunlight from reaching sediment.
- **c.** Managers increase oyster density without monitoring.
- **d.** An oyster intervention improves one water-quality measure but triggers a bottom-algae problem, so managers broaden monitoring and adjust density. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — An oyster intervention improves one water-quality measure but triggers a bottom-algae problem, so managers broaden monitoring and adjust density.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Progress check · Enhanced ACT progress check · Form delta · delta-reading-31

**Stimulus:** Sculptor Jo Adebayo began each wood panel with a geometric plan, but she did not force every line to follow it. When a dense knot deflected the chisel, Adebayo traced the grain around it and repeated that curve elsewhere in the panel. The obstacle became a motif. Students watching her assumed improvisation meant abandoning the plan. Adebayo instead described it as negotiation: the original geometry set a direction, while the wood supplied local information. The finished panel remained structured, but its repeated curves recorded decisions made in response to one particular board.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Adebayo discards every plan before carving.
- **b.** Adebayo preserves an overall geometric plan while using the wood's grain and knots to shape local design decisions. **(correct)**
- **c.** Dense knots make wood impossible to carve.
- **d.** Students design the entire panel for Adebayo.

**Correct answer:** b — Adebayo preserves an overall geometric plan while using the wood's grain and knots to shape local design decisions.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-01

**Stimulus:** When Nia inherited her aunt's orchard notebook, she expected tidy harvest totals. Instead, many pages held sketches of bruised fruit, dates of sudden frosts, and arrows connecting one season to the next. At first the marks looked like a record of failure. Nia compared them with the orchard's planting map and noticed a pattern: trees near the low stone wall bloomed early and were repeatedly damaged by cold air that settled there overnight. Her aunt had gradually moved the most sensitive varieties uphill. The notebook did not contain a finished rule. It preserved the observations from which a rule could be built. Nia used that evidence to choose where to plant six new trees, then added her own notes after the next frost.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Nia turns an apparent record of orchard failures into evidence for future planting decisions. **(correct)**
- **b.** Nia proves that every fruit tree should be planted uphill.
- **c.** Nia replaces her aunt's notes with a more organized harvest ledger.
- **d.** Nia discovers that stone walls always damage nearby trees.

**Correct answer:** a — Nia turns an apparent record of orchard failures into evidence for future planting decisions.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

**Stimulus:** When Nia inherited her aunt's orchard notebook, she expected tidy harvest totals. Instead, many pages held sketches of bruised fruit, dates of sudden frosts, and arrows connecting one season to the next. At first the marks looked like a record of failure. Nia compared them with the orchard's planting map and noticed a pattern: trees near the low stone wall bloomed early and were repeatedly damaged by cold air that settled there overnight. Her aunt had gradually moved the most sensitive varieties uphill. The notebook did not contain a finished rule. It preserved the observations from which a rule could be built. Nia used that evidence to choose where to plant six new trees, then added her own notes after the next frost.

**Question:** Which inference is best supported by the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Nia's aunt intended to publish the notebook.
- **b.** Nia had managed the orchard before inheriting it.
- **c.** Nia's aunt treated unsuccessful seasons as information worth preserving. **(correct)**
- **d.** The aunt believed frost could be eliminated entirely.

**Correct answer:** c — Nia's aunt treated unsuccessful seasons as information worth preserving.

**Explanation:** The inference follows from the sequence of actions and evidence while avoiding an unsupported motive or absolute claim.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-07

**Stimulus:** Rail inspectors have long checked bridge fasteners by sight, but a research group tested whether sound could reveal changes too small to see. They attached inexpensive microphones to a scale bridge and recorded the vibrations produced as a cart crossed it. Tight fasteners created a short, high-frequency response. After the researchers loosened one bolt by a quarter turn, a lower vibration persisted longer. The team did not claim that microphones should replace visual inspections. Wind and passing traffic can distort recordings, and a sound change does not identify the exact defect. Instead, the system could flag a location for closer inspection. In that role, sound becomes an early warning rather than a final diagnosis.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Microphones can identify every bridge defect without inspectors.
- **b.** Visual bridge inspections are too inaccurate to continue.
- **c.** Sound monitoring may help inspectors locate possible bridge problems that still require direct examination. **(correct)**
- **d.** Loose bolts always produce the same sound in every setting.

**Correct answer:** c — Sound monitoring may help inspectors locate possible bridge problems that still require direct examination.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

**Correct answer:** d — A lower vibration lasted longer.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-13

**Stimulus:** Conservator A: When I repair a cracked ceramic vessel, I try to make the join quiet but not invisible. A visitor should first see the vessel's form, yet a close look should reveal where modern material begins. Hiding every trace of repair can make a restored object seem untouched by time.<br><br>Conservator B: I also document every addition, but I place that information in the display label rather than on the object's surface. A visible join can interrupt a painted pattern that the original maker intended as continuous. When reliable evidence shows how the pattern crossed the break, I favor a visually integrated repair.<br><br>Both conservators reject undocumented restoration. Their disagreement concerns where the evidence of repair should remain visible: on the vessel itself or in the record beside it.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The conservators agree repairs must be documented but differ about whether the repair should remain visible on the object. **(correct)**
- **b.** Both conservators believe every crack should be left unrepaired.
- **c.** Conservator A values painted patterns while Conservator B ignores them.
- **d.** The conservators disagree about whether museums should display ceramics.

**Correct answer:** a — The conservators agree repairs must be documented but differ about whether the repair should remain visible on the object.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-19

**Stimulus:** A coastal town wanted to reopen a footpath erased by decades of dune movement. No single map showed the entire route. Surveyor Mateo Silva compared a 1912 harbor chart, aerial photographs from the 1950s, and property descriptions that referred to a 'cart track above the high-water mark.' Each source had a different distortion. The chart simplified curves; the photographs showed vegetation but not legal boundaries; the descriptions used landmarks that no longer existed. Silva treated the sources as overlapping clues rather than competing verdicts. Where all three pointed to the same ridge, the town marked a provisional route. Archaeologists then tested those locations and found compacted shell and gravel beneath the sand. The recovered path was not copied from one perfect record. It emerged from agreement among imperfect ones.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The oldest harbor chart provides a complete and exact route.
- **b.** Archaeologists prove that property descriptions are never useful.
- **c.** The town reconstructs the footpath by combining partial evidence from several imperfect sources. **(correct)**
- **d.** Dune movement has made coastal paths impossible to recover.

**Correct answer:** c — The town reconstructs the footpath by combining partial evidence from several imperfect sources.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-25

**Stimulus:** Farmers in one valley welcomed the copper ground beetle because it consumed larvae that damaged bean roots. The beetle rarely flew and seemed unlikely to spread far. A decade later, however, biologists found it in upland meadows beyond the farms. Genetic comparisons suggested that beetles had repeatedly traveled in soil attached to nursery plants. In the meadows, the insects ate not only pest larvae but also the larvae of a rare native moth. Removing every beetle was impractical, so managers changed nursery rules, cleaned soil from equipment, and concentrated trapping near newly planted sites. The same appetite that made the beetle useful in fields made it risky elsewhere. Its effect depended less on whether it was 'helpful' or 'harmful' than on which community it entered.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** A beetle useful on farms becomes a conservation risk after human-assisted movement carries it into a different ecosystem. **(correct)**
- **b.** The beetle stopped eating crop pests after ten years.
- **c.** Rare moths caused the beetle to spread from upland meadows.
- **d.** Managers successfully removed every beetle from the valley.

**Correct answer:** a — A beetle useful on farms becomes a conservation risk after human-assisted movement carries it into a different ecosystem.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

- **c.** Inside harvested bean pods.
- **d.** Along streams during floods.

**Correct answer:** b — In soil transported with nursery plants.

**Explanation:** The correct choice restates the specific detail supplied in the passage; the other choices change or invent that evidence.
