- **b.** Managers believe trapping on farms is unnecessary because no beetles remain.
- **c.** The managers' strategy emphasizes preventing new introductions and responding early. **(correct)**
- **d.** Managers regard nursery practices as unrelated to spread.

**Correct answer:** c — The managers' strategy emphasizes preventing new introductions and responding early.

**Explanation:** The inference follows from the sequence of actions and evidence while avoiding an unsupported motive or absolute claim.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-28

**Stimulus:** Farmers in one valley welcomed the copper ground beetle because it consumed larvae that damaged bean roots. The beetle rarely flew and seemed unlikely to spread far. A decade later, however, biologists found it in upland meadows beyond the farms. Genetic comparisons suggested that beetles had repeatedly traveled in soil attached to nursery plants. In the meadows, the insects ate not only pest larvae but also the larvae of a rare native moth. Removing every beetle was impractical, so managers changed nursery rules, cleaned soil from equipment, and concentrated trapping near newly planted sites. The same appetite that made the beetle useful in fields made it risky elsewhere. Its effect depended less on whether it was 'helpful' or 'harmful' than on which community it entered.

**Question:** The final sentence primarily serves to:

**Choices:**

- **a.** It claims the beetle has changed its diet permanently.
- **b.** It argues that crop pests should be protected.
- **c.** It shows that the moth is harmful on farms.
- **d.** It explains why a single label cannot describe the beetle's effect in every ecological setting. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — It explains why a single label cannot describe the beetle's effect in every ecological setting.

**Explanation:** The final sentence interprets the preceding evidence and completes the passage's central contrast or method.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form atlas · atlas-reading-31

**Stimulus:** Choreographer Sora Bell's rehearsal scripts contain few descriptions of finished movements. Their margins are crowded instead with verbs: suspend, ricochet, resist, fold. Dancer Emil Reyes initially treated the words as names for fixed shapes. Bell asked him to perform the same verb three ways—slowly with the whole body, sharply with one arm, and while moving backward. The resulting phrases looked different, yet each preserved a recognizable quality. Bell then arranged the phrases beside one another, using the contrast to build a duet. For her, the margin verbs were not instructions that narrowed a dancer's choices. They were constraints that focused invention. By limiting the kind of energy a phrase explored, each word opened several possible forms.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Bell expects dancers to copy movements exactly from written diagrams.
- **b.** Reyes proves that action words have only one physical meaning.
- **c.** Bell uses action words as focused constraints that generate varied movement rather than prescribe one fixed shape. **(correct)**
- **d.** Bell removes every constraint before creating a duet.

**Correct answer:** c — Bell uses action words as focused constraints that generate varied movement rather than prescribe one fixed shape.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-01

**Stimulus:** A box of water-stained harbor logs seemed useless to engineer Amara Wells because many columns were incomplete. Alongside arrival times, however, keepers had noted unusual vibrations in the wooden pier and sketched the direction of strong winds. Wells aligned those notes with surviving repair bills. She found that braces on the west side were replaced most often after weeks of crosswind, even when no single storm was severe. The logs did not provide a modern stress calculation. They preserved repeated observations that revealed where small forces accumulated. Wells used the pattern to place sensors on the rebuilt pier and continued the record with digital measurements.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Wells proves that one storm destroyed the original pier.
- **b.** Wells combines informal historical observations with repair records to guide modern monitoring of a pier. **(correct)**
- **c.** The harbor logs contain complete engineering calculations.
- **d.** Digital sensors make all historical records unnecessary.

**Correct answer:** b — Wells combines informal historical observations with repair records to guide modern monitoring of a pier.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-07

**Stimulus:** Satellite maps show which neighborhoods absorb the most sunlight during the day, but they reveal less about how quickly individual streets cool after sunset. A research team mounted temperature sensors on city buses that followed fixed evening routes. Readings showed that broad avenues cooled rapidly when wind moved through them, while narrow courtyards remained warm hours longer. The team compared those patterns with building height and pavement type. It did not recommend the same intervention everywhere: shade trees mattered on exposed avenues, whereas ventilation corridors mattered more in enclosed blocks. The bus data added timing and street-scale detail to the regional map.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Satellite maps should never be used to study cities.
- **b.** Every hot location needs the same number of trees.
- **c.** Buses caused narrow courtyards to remain warm.
- **d.** Evening bus measurements add local timing information that helps tailor heat interventions to different street conditions. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — Evening bus measurements add local timing information that helps tailor heat interventions to different street conditions.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-13

**Stimulus:** Editor A: In a historical novel, I preserve a character's regional grammar when it shapes voice or social relationships. I may remove spellings that make every word difficult to decode, but I will not flatten a distinctive sentence pattern merely because it differs from formal prose.<br><br>Editor B: I also protect voice, yet I worry when unfamiliar grammar forces readers to solve the language before they can follow the scene. I prefer a lighter signal: a few recurring words and rhythms, supported by context.<br><br>Both editors reject replacing every feature with standard prose. They differ mainly in how much sustained unfamiliarity they believe a reader can navigate without losing the story.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Both editors want to remove every trace of dialect.
- **b.** The editors agree dialect can convey voice but differ over how densely unfamiliar features should appear. **(correct)**
- **c.** Editor A values plot while Editor B values only grammar.
- **d.** The editors disagree about whether novels need characters.

**Correct answer:** b — The editors agree dialect can convey voice but differ over how densely unfamiliar features should appear.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-18

**Stimulus:** Editor A: In a historical novel, I preserve a character's regional grammar when it shapes voice or social relationships. I may remove spellings that make every word difficult to decode, but I will not flatten a distinctive sentence pattern merely because it differs from formal prose.<br><br>Editor B: I also protect voice, yet I worry when unfamiliar grammar forces readers to solve the language before they can follow the scene. I prefer a lighter signal: a few recurring words and rhythms, supported by context.<br><br>Both editors reject replacing every feature with standard prose. They differ mainly in how much sustained unfamiliarity they believe a reader can navigate without losing the story.

**Question:** Which new finding would most strongly support the passage's central interpretation?

**Choices:**

- **a.** The novel has a colorful cover.
- **b.** Some characters speak more often than others.
- **c.** Readers follow the scene more accurately when recurring dialect cues are supported by clear context than when every word uses unfamiliar spelling. **(correct)**
- **d.** Formal prose uses paragraphs.

**Correct answer:** c — Readers follow the scene more accurately when recurring dialect cues are supported by clear context than when every word uses unfamiliar spelling.

**Explanation:** The correct finding directly tests and reinforces the passage's explanation; the other facts may be true but do not bear on that explanation.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-19

**Stimulus:** Historians knew that a nineteenth-century canal once crossed the eastern field, but plowing had erased its visible edges. Priya Nordin compared tax maps, workers' diaries, and differences in soil moisture visible in drone images. Each source narrowed the search in a different way. Maps gave legal parcels but simplified the canal's curves. Diaries mentioned walking times from a mill whose foundation survived. Moisture patterns traced buried stone that retained water. Where the clues converged, excavation uncovered an iron hinge and a line of dressed blocks—the remains of a control gate. No source had supplied the answer alone.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** A single tax map shows the exact canal gate.
- **b.** Drone images make excavation unnecessary.
- **c.** Workers' diaries describe how the hinge was manufactured.
- **d.** Researchers locate a lost canal gate by combining spatial, written, and physical evidence. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — Researchers locate a lost canal gate by combining spatial, written, and physical evidence.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-25

**Stimulus:** Biologists installed floating rafts to give young native fish shelter from shoreline predators. Within two seasons, a nonnative mussel covered the rafts' submerged ropes. The mussels filtered the water, making it clearer, but they also concentrated nutrients beneath each raft. Dense algae then grew on the bottom and reduced oxygen at night. Removing the rafts would eliminate fish shelter, so the team tested rope coatings and shorter cleaning intervals. The project had not simply succeeded or failed. One structure produced a benefit and a new pathway for harm, requiring the design to change.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Clearer water always improves oxygen levels.
- **b.** A fish-shelter project creates an unintended mussel problem, leading biologists to modify rather than abandon the design. **(correct)**
- **c.** The team removes every raft immediately.
- **d.** Native fish cause algae to grow beneath the rafts.

**Correct answer:** b — A fish-shelter project creates an unintended mussel problem, leading biologists to modify rather than abandon the design.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-31

**Stimulus:** Composer Devon Li recorded a violin phrase in four rooms: a carpeted studio, a tiled stairwell, a wooden hall, and an empty warehouse. Rather than remove each room's reverberation, Li treated the echoes as musical material. A short note from the stairwell became a bright cluster; a sustained warehouse tone formed a slowly fading background. During rehearsal, musicians first tried to imitate the recordings exactly. Li instead asked them to respond to the length and density of each echo. The performance therefore combined fixed recordings with live decisions. The rooms had shaped the source sounds, but they did not dictate a single response.

**Question:** Which choice best states the central idea of the passage?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Li removes all reverberation before composing.
- **b.** The musicians perform only in the warehouse.
- **c.** Each room produces exactly the same violin sound.
- **d.** Li uses room echoes as constraints that guide, but do not fully determine, live musical responses. **(correct)**

**Correct answer:** d — Li uses room echoes as constraints that guide, but do not fully determine, live musical responses.

**Explanation:** The correct choice accounts for the passage's problem, evidence, and outcome without adding a claim the passage does not make.

### Full-length test · Enhanced ACT full-length core practice · Form beacon · beacon-reading-36

**Stimulus:** Composer Devon Li recorded a violin phrase in four rooms: a carpeted studio, a tiled stairwell, a wooden hall, and an empty warehouse. Rather than remove each room's reverberation, Li treated the echoes as musical material. A short note from the stairwell became a bright cluster; a sustained warehouse tone formed a slowly fading background. During rehearsal, musicians first tried to imitate the recordings exactly. Li instead asked them to respond to the length and density of each echo. The performance therefore combined fixed recordings with live decisions. The rooms had shaped the source sounds, but they did not dictate a single response.

**Question:** Which new finding would most strongly support the passage's central interpretation?

**Choices:**

- **a.** Different ensembles make distinct live choices while listeners can still match each response to its source echo. **(correct)**
- **b.** The violin has four strings.
- **c.** The warehouse is the largest room.
- **d.** Carpet is softer than tile.

**Correct answer:** a — Different ensembles make distinct live choices while listeners can still match each response to its source echo.

**Explanation:** The correct finding directly tests and reinforces the passage's explanation; the other facts may be true but do not bear on that explanation.
